People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is… I’ve just kind of stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but things that used to feel like they were mine, like part of who I was, just don’t pull me in anymore.

Like, I’ve got all my fishing gear still sitting in the garage. Normally I’d be checking it, organizing it, thinking about where to go next, messing with the tackle boxes even when I didn’t have a trip planned. Now I walk past it and it’s like looking at someone else’s stuff. Same with my woodshop. I used to lose hours out there, sanding or cutting or planning some little project, and now even the thought of going out there feels like another chore I’m supposed to do.

And my kids will ask me to play chess, which used to be our thing, and I’ll sit there with them, but I’m not really there. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions, trying not to make it obvious that I’m not enjoying it. That’s probably what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s not just one thing. It’s like the interest went out of everything.

Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.

It’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.

I used to lose hours out there, sanding or cutting or planning some little project, and now even the thought of going out there feels like another chore I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd my kids will ask me to play chess, which used to be our thing, and I’ll sit there with them, but I’m not really there. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions, trying not to make it obvious that I’m not enjoying it. That’s probably what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s not just one thing. It’s like the interest went out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.\n\nDoctor: For most of the past year and a half, have you felt down or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know I haven’t felt right for a long time, and there are a lot of days where I feel pretty low, but “nearly every day” makes me stop and think because I don’t really track it that way.\n\nIt’s more like everything has been kind of covered over, if that makes sense. Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.

It’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.

And then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.

I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.

I mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. Now I walk past it and it’s like looking at someone else’s stuff. Same with my woodshop. I used to lose hours out there, sanding or cutting or planning some little project, and now even the thought of going out there feels like another chore I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd my kids will ask me to play chess, which used to be our thing, and I’ll sit there with them, but I’m not really there. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions, trying not to make it obvious that I’m not enjoying it. That’s probably what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s not just one thing. It’s like the interest went out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.\n\nDoctor: For most of the past year and a half, have you felt down or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know I haven’t felt right for a long time, and there are a lot of days where I feel pretty low, but “nearly every day” makes me stop and think because I don’t really track it that way.\n\nIt’s more like everything has been kind of covered over, if that makes sense. Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It feels like it’s been forever, honestly, but if I’m being accurate, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems really changed everything at home. That’s the marker in my head, because before that I still felt like I had some normal parts of my life, and after that everything slowly got rearranged around what was happening with her. So… continuous, yeah, more or less since then, but not a full two years yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. It has. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.

I’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.

There was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”

And at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.

Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It feels like it’s been forever, honestly, but if I’m being accurate, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems really changed everything at home. That’s the marker in my head, because before that I still felt like I had some normal parts of my life, and after that everything slowly got rearranged around what was happening with her. So… continuous, yeah, more or less since then, but not a full two years yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”

It’s like the interest went out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.\n\nDoctor: For most of the past year and a half, have you felt down or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know I haven’t felt right for a long time, and there are a lot of days where I feel pretty low, but “nearly every day” makes me stop and think because I don’t really track it that way.\n\nIt’s more like everything has been kind of covered over, if that makes sense. Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It feels like it’s been forever, honestly, but if I’m being accurate, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems really changed everything at home. That’s the marker in my head, because before that I still felt like I had some normal parts of my life, and after that everything slowly got rearranged around what was happening with her. So… continuous, yeah, more or less since then, but not a full two years yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”\n\nI know I feel slowed down a lot of the time, but whether it’s something other people can actually see, I don’t know. My wife might notice things before I do, but I can’t think of a specific time where someone pointed it out that way. At work, people have noticed I’m not as on top of things, but that’s not exactly the same as moving or speaking slowly.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.

Most days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. I’ll know I should eat, especially if I’m going to be on a job site all day, but breakfast is easy for me to just skip now. I used to at least grab something, even if it was quick, but lately I’ll just have coffee or nothing and keep moving.

Dinner’s the same kind of thing. My wife will make food, or there’ll be food there, and I’ll sit down with everyone, but I’m more picking at it than eating. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight. I just don’t feel like eating, and when I do, it doesn’t feel worth the effort half the time.

And I’ve noticed it physically too. My belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. I almost didn’t pay attention to it at first, but then you keep using a tighter hole on the belt and it’s kind of hard to pretend it’s nothing.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… I’ve just kind of stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but things that used to feel like they were mine, like part of who I was, just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nLike, I’ve got all my fishing gear still sitting in the garage. Normally I’d be checking it, organizing it, thinking about where to go next, messing with the tackle boxes even when I didn’t have a trip planned. Now I walk past it and it’s like looking at someone else’s stuff. Same with my woodshop. I used to lose hours out there, sanding or cutting or planning some little project, and now even the thought of going out there feels like another chore I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd my kids will ask me to play chess, which used to be our thing, and I’ll sit there with them, but I’m not really there. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions, trying not to make it obvious that I’m not enjoying it. That’s probably what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s not just one thing. It’s like the interest went out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.\n\nDoctor: For most of the past year and a half, have you felt down or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know I haven’t felt right for a long time, and there are a lot of days where I feel pretty low, but “nearly every day” makes me stop and think because I don’t really track it that way.\n\nIt’s more like everything has been kind of covered over, if that makes sense. Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It feels like it’s been forever, honestly, but if I’m being accurate, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems really changed everything at home. That’s the marker in my head, because before that I still felt like I had some normal parts of my life, and after that everything slowly got rearranged around what was happening with her. So… continuous, yeah, more or less since then, but not a full two years yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”\n\nI know I feel slowed down a lot of the time, but whether it’s something other people can actually see, I don’t know. My wife might notice things before I do, but I can’t think of a specific time where someone pointed it out that way. At work, people have noticed I’m not as on top of things, but that’s not exactly the same as moving or speaking slowly.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a noticeable drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.\n\nMost days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”\n\nI know I feel slowed down a lot of the time, but whether it’s something other people can actually see, I don’t know. My wife might notice things before I do, but I can’t think of a specific time where someone pointed it out that way. At work, people have noticed I’m not as on top of things, but that’s not exactly the same as moving or speaking slowly.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a noticeable drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.\n\nMost days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. I’ll know I should eat, especially if I’m going to be on a job site all day, but breakfast is easy for me to just skip now. I used to at least grab something, even if it was quick, but lately I’ll just have coffee or nothing and keep moving.\n\nDinner’s the same kind of thing. My wife will make food, or there’ll be food there, and I’ll sit down with everyone, but I’m more picking at it than eating. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight. I just don’t feel like eating, and when I do, it doesn’t feel worth the effort half the time.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. I almost didn’t pay attention to it at first, but then you keep using a tighter hole on the belt and it’s kind of hard to pretend it’s nothing.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this started, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in almost everything nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / construction_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can point to a clear time like that, no.

I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before — everybody does, I guess — times where work was bad or I was stressed or kind of worn down. But if you’re asking whether there was a period before this where, nearly every day for two weeks or more, I felt depressed or like I lost interest in almost everything… I’m not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that I’d confidently describe that way.

I don’t want to say absolutely never, because memory gets a little fuzzy when you’re trying to compare it to what’s happening now. But I don’t remember anything this persistent or this all-consuming before. This feels different enough that it’s hard for me to match it to some earlier episode.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… I’ve just kind of stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but things that used to feel like they were mine, like part of who I was, just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nLike, I’ve got all my fishing gear still sitting in the garage. Normally I’d be checking it, organizing it, thinking about where to go next, messing with the tackle boxes even when I didn’t have a trip planned. Now I walk past it and it’s like looking at someone else’s stuff. Same with my woodshop. I used to lose hours out there, sanding or cutting or planning some little project, and now even the thought of going out there feels like another chore I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd my kids will ask me to play chess, which used to be our thing, and I’ll sit there with them, but I’m not really there. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions, trying not to make it obvious that I’m not enjoying it. That’s probably what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s not just one thing. It’s like the interest went out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.\n\nDoctor: For most of the past year and a half, have you felt down or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know I haven’t felt right for a long time, and there are a lot of days where I feel pretty low, but “nearly every day” makes me stop and think because I don’t really track it that way.\n\nIt’s more like everything has been kind of covered over, if that makes sense. Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It feels like it’s been forever, honestly, but if I’m being accurate, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems really changed everything at home. That’s the marker in my head, because before that I still felt like I had some normal parts of my life, and after that everything slowly got rearranged around what was happening with her. So… continuous, yeah, more or less since then, but not a full two years yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I guess the main thing is that I just stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to—my fishing gear is still sitting untouched in the garage, the woodshop feels like a chore, and even when my kids try to get me to play chess I’m just going through the motions.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Most days food tastes like cardboard, so I’ll skip breakfast and pick at dinner even if I’ve been on a job site all day, and my belt has had to come in a couple notches without me trying.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: I can get into bed exhausted, but I either lie there running through bills and schedules or I wake up around three or four in the morning and stare at the ceiling until it’s time to drive to the site.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: By midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift, and even walking the project or climbing stairs with the crew feels like my body is carrying wet concrete.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I’ve failed my wife, my kids, and the guys at work, and if anything goes wrong—a missed appointment, a cost overrun, a safety issue—I take it as proof that I’m useless and everyone would be better off without me in charge.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I used to keep schedules and subcontractors straight in my head, but now I’ll read the same email three times, forget what I was about to say in meetings, and freeze over decisions I used to make in five minutes.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: On those early drives before anyone else is awake, I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there, and sometimes the thought of steering off the road scares me because it feels too close.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve started missing things at work that could affect deadlines and safety, and after the near-miss I couldn’t pretend it was just stress anymore. At home I’m present physically, but my wife and kids are having to work around me instead of being able to count on me the way they used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and seven months, since my wife’s health problems changed everything at home.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / construction_manager / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”\n\nI know I feel slowed down a lot of the time, but whether it’s something other people can actually see, I don’t know. My wife might notice things before I do, but I can’t think of a specific time where someone pointed it out that way. At work, people have noticed I’m not as on top of things, but that’s not exactly the same as moving or speaking slowly.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a noticeable drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.\n\nMost days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. I’ll know I should eat, especially if I’m going to be on a job site all day, but breakfast is easy for me to just skip now. I used to at least grab something, even if it was quick, but lately I’ll just have coffee or nothing and keep moving.\n\nDinner’s the same kind of thing. My wife will make food, or there’ll be food there, and I’ll sit down with everyone, but I’m more picking at it than eating. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight. I just don’t feel like eating, and when I do, it doesn’t feel worth the effort half the time.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. I almost didn’t pay attention to it at first, but then you keep using a tighter hole on the belt and it’s kind of hard to pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in almost everything nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to a clear time like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough stretches before — everybody does, I guess — times where work was bad or I was stressed or kind of worn down. But if you’re asking whether there was a period before this where, nearly every day for two weeks or more, I felt depressed or like I lost interest in almost everything… I’m not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that I’d confidently describe that way.\n\nI don’t want to say absolutely never, because memory gets a little fuzzy when you’re trying to compare it to what’s happening now. But I don’t remember anything this persistent or this all-consuming before. This feels different enough that it’s hard for me to match it to some earlier episode.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling tired or like you have very little energy nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Nearly every day, honestly.

It’s not just normal tired, like after a long day on site. It’s more like I wake up already behind. I’ll get going because I have to, but by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And that’s before the day has really even gotten started.

Walking the project feels harder than it should. Climbing stairs with the crew, going back and forth across the site, even just standing there trying to keep up with what everyone’s saying — my body feels heavy, like I’m carrying wet concrete around with me. That’s the best way I can explain it. Everything takes more effort than it used to.

And I keep trying to tell myself, you know, “Just push through, you’ve done harder days than this,” but it doesn’t feel like the kind of tired that goes away once I get moving. It just kind of sits there all day.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… I’ve just kind of stopped caring about everything I used to look forward to. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but things that used to feel like they were mine, like part of who I was, just don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nLike, I’ve got all my fishing gear still sitting in the garage. Normally I’d be checking it, organizing it, thinking about where to go next, messing with the tackle boxes even when I didn’t have a trip planned. Now I walk past it and it’s like looking at someone else’s stuff. Same with my woodshop. I used to lose hours out there, sanding or cutting or planning some little project, and now even the thought of going out there feels like another chore I’m supposed to do.\n\nAnd my kids will ask me to play chess, which used to be our thing, and I’ll sit there with them, but I’m not really there. I’m just moving pieces around, going through the motions, trying not to make it obvious that I’m not enjoying it. That’s probably what finally made me feel like I should come in, because it’s not just one thing. It’s like the interest went out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It wasn’t like one single day where everything just snapped, but it really traces back to when my wife’s health problems started changing everything at home. That’s when life kind of stopped being the way it had been.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and seven months now, give or take. I remember because at first I kept telling myself it was just a rough patch, like once we adjusted or got through the next appointment or the next change, I’d feel more like myself again. But it just kept stretching on. The house, the routines, what I had time and energy for — all of it shifted around her being sick, and somewhere in that stretch I just sort of… lost the thread. So yeah, I’d say it started after that major change, and it’s been going on ever since.\n\nDoctor: For most of the past year and a half, have you felt down or depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know I haven’t felt right for a long time, and there are a lot of days where I feel pretty low, but “nearly every day” makes me stop and think because I don’t really track it that way.\n\nIt’s more like everything has been kind of covered over, if that makes sense. Some days I can function and talk to people and get through what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t know if I would’ve called it depressed in the moment. But if you’re asking whether this heavy, down feeling has been hanging around most of the time… yeah, I think it has. I just don’t know if I can honestly say I’ve paid close enough attention to count the days.\n\nDoctor: Have these low feelings and loss of interest been going on continuously for two years or more?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It feels like it’s been forever, honestly, but if I’m being accurate, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems really changed everything at home. That’s the marker in my head, because before that I still felt like I had some normal parts of my life, and after that everything slowly got rearranged around what was happening with her. So… continuous, yeah, more or less since then, but not a full two years yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some detailed plan all the time or anything like that, but there are these moments where the thought just shows up, and it scares me how normal it can feel for a second. Especially on the early drives, when I’m up before everyone else and the roads are quiet and it’s still dark out. I’ll be driving and I’ll catch myself thinking… it would be easier if I just didn’t make it there. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”\n\nI know I feel slowed down a lot of the time, but whether it’s something other people can actually see, I don’t know. My wife might notice things before I do, but I can’t think of a specific time where someone pointed it out that way. At work, people have noticed I’m not as on top of things, but that’s not exactly the same as moving or speaking slowly.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a noticeable drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.\n\nMost days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. I’ll know I should eat, especially if I’m going to be on a job site all day, but breakfast is easy for me to just skip now. I used to at least grab something, even if it was quick, but lately I’ll just have coffee or nothing and keep moving.\n\nDinner’s the same kind of thing. My wife will make food, or there’ll be food there, and I’ll sit down with everyone, but I’m more picking at it than eating. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight. I just don’t feel like eating, and when I do, it doesn’t feel worth the effort half the time.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. I almost didn’t pay attention to it at first, but then you keep using a tighter hole on the belt and it’s kind of hard to pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in almost everything nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to a clear time like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough stretches before — everybody does, I guess — times where work was bad or I was stressed or kind of worn down. Nothing is coming to mind that I’d confidently describe that way.\n\nI don’t want to say absolutely never, because memory gets a little fuzzy when you’re trying to compare it to what’s happening now. Nearly every day, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like after a long day on site. It’s more like I wake up already behind. Like if something happened and I didn’t have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd then sometimes it goes a step further in my head, like the thought of steering off the road comes up, and that’s the part that really scares me, because it feels too close. I don’t want to say I want to do it exactly, but the thought is there, and it doesn’t feel as distant as I wish it did.\n\nDoctor: Before this started a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches before, like anybody, I guess. Times where I was stressed or worn down or not really enjoying much for a bit. But when you say “at least two weeks” and feeling depressed or losing interest in things, I don’t know that I ever thought about it that way before all of this started.\n\nNothing really stands out in my mind as, like, a clear episode where I would’ve said, “Something is seriously wrong with me,” if that’s what you mean. But I also wasn’t exactly keeping track back then. I probably would’ve just told myself to push through it and go to work and not make a thing of it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t point to a definite earlier period like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has. More than I wanted to admit for a long time.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I used to be pretty good at keeping them in my head — schedules, crews, deliveries, inspections, safety stuff, who needs to be where and when. Lately I’ve been missing things I shouldn’t miss. Not just little paperwork things, though there’s been that too. I mean things that can affect deadlines, and honestly some things that could affect safety if somebody else doesn’t catch them.\n\nThere was a near-miss at work, and after that I couldn’t really keep telling myself it was just stress or that I was tired and needed to push through. I don’t want to make it sound like I caused some disaster, but it shook me because I thought, “Okay, this isn’t just me having a bad attitude. I’m not tracking things the way I need to be tracking them.”\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same pattern, just in a different way. I’m there physically, but I’m not really… useful the way I used to be. My wife and the kids have to work around me more than they can count on me. That’s hard to say, because I used to be someone people could depend on. Now it feels like everyone’s adjusting to whether I’m going to forget something, or be checked out, or not follow through. So yes, it’s definitely making work harder, and it’s not staying at work either.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had anyone say it to me in those words, like, “You’re moving slower” or “You’re talking slower.”\n\nI know I feel slowed down a lot of the time, but whether it’s something other people can actually see, I don’t know. My wife might notice things before I do, but I can’t think of a specific time where someone pointed it out that way. At work, people have noticed I’m not as on top of things, but that’s not exactly the same as moving or speaking slowly.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a noticeable drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.\n\nMost days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. I’ll know I should eat, especially if I’m going to be on a job site all day, but breakfast is easy for me to just skip now. I used to at least grab something, even if it was quick, but lately I’ll just have coffee or nothing and keep moving.\n\nDinner’s the same kind of thing. My wife will make food, or there’ll be food there, and I’ll sit down with everyone, but I’m more picking at it than eating. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight. I just don’t feel like eating, and when I do, it doesn’t feel worth the effort half the time.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. I almost didn’t pay attention to it at first, but then you keep using a tighter hole on the belt and it’s kind of hard to pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in almost everything nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to a clear time like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough stretches before — everybody does, I guess — times where work was bad or I was stressed or kind of worn down. But if you’re asking whether there was a period before this where, nearly every day for two weeks or more, I felt depressed or like I lost interest in almost everything… I’m not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that I’d confidently describe that way.\n\nI don’t want to say absolutely never, because memory gets a little fuzzy when you’re trying to compare it to what’s happening now. But I don’t remember anything this persistent or this all-consuming before. This feels different enough that it’s hard for me to match it to some earlier episode.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or like you have very little energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Nearly every day, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like after a long day on site. It’s more like I wake up already behind. I’ll get going because I have to, but by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And that’s before the day has really even gotten started.\n\nWalking the project feels harder than it should. Climbing stairs with the crew, going back and forth across the site, even just standing there trying to keep up with what everyone’s saying — my body feels heavy, like I’m carrying wet concrete around with me. That’s the best way I can explain it. Everything takes more effort than it used to.\n\nAnd I keep trying to tell myself, you know, “Just push through, you’ve done harder days than this,” but it doesn’t feel like the kind of tired that goes away once I get moving. It just kind of sits there all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Sleep’s been… pretty bad.

The strange thing is I’ll be exhausted by the time I get into bed, like my body feels completely done, and I’ll think, “Okay, at least I’ll pass out tonight.” But then I lie there and my head just won’t shut off. I’ll start running through bills, schedules, what has to happen on the job site the next day, who’s waiting on what, what I forgot to do, all of that. It’s like as soon as the room gets quiet, everything I’ve been trying not to think about shows up at once.

And if I do fall asleep, a lot of times I’m waking up around three or four in the morning. Not even for a clear reason. I just wake up and stare at the ceiling, and then I’m aware of every minute going by until it’s time to get up and drive to the site. I’ll tell myself not to look at the clock, but then I do, and it just makes it worse.

So yeah, I’m in bed, but I don’t feel like I’m really resting much.

But I can’t honestly say yes, other people have noticed that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a noticeable drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah. My appetite’s definitely dropped.\n\nMost days food just doesn’t really… taste like anything. Cardboard is the word that comes to mind. I’ll know I should eat, especially if I’m going to be on a job site all day, but breakfast is easy for me to just skip now. I used to at least grab something, even if it was quick, but lately I’ll just have coffee or nothing and keep moving.\n\nDinner’s the same kind of thing. My wife will make food, or there’ll be food there, and I’ll sit down with everyone, but I’m more picking at it than eating. It’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight. I just don’t feel like eating, and when I do, it doesn’t feel worth the effort half the time.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My belt’s had to come in a couple notches, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. I almost didn’t pay attention to it at first, but then you keep using a tighter hole on the belt and it’s kind of hard to pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in almost everything nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to a clear time like that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough stretches before — everybody does, I guess — times where work was bad or I was stressed or kind of worn down. But if you’re asking whether there was a period before this where, nearly every day for two weeks or more, I felt depressed or like I lost interest in almost everything… I’m not sure. Nothing is coming to mind that I’d confidently describe that way.\n\nI don’t want to say absolutely never, because memory gets a little fuzzy when you’re trying to compare it to what’s happening now. But I don’t remember anything this persistent or this all-consuming before. This feels different enough that it’s hard for me to match it to some earlier episode.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or like you have very little energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Nearly every day, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like after a long day on site. It’s more like I wake up already behind. I’ll get going because I have to, but by midmorning I feel like I’ve already worked a full shift. And that’s before the day has really even gotten started.\n\nWalking the project feels harder than it should. Climbing stairs with the crew, going back and forth across the site, even just standing there trying to keep up with what everyone’s saying — my body feels heavy, like I’m carrying wet concrete around with me. That’s the best way I can explain it. Everything takes more effort than it used to.\n\nAnd I keep trying to tell myself, you know, “Just push through, you’ve done harder days than this,” but it doesn’t feel like the kind of tired that goes away once I get moving. It just kind of sits there all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep’s been… pretty bad.\n\nThe strange thing is I’ll be exhausted by the time I get into bed, like my body feels completely done, and I’ll think, “Okay, at least I’ll pass out tonight.” But then I lie there and my head just won’t shut off. I’ll start running through bills, schedules, what has to happen on the job site the next day, who’s waiting on what, what I forgot to do, all of that. It’s like as soon as the room gets quiet, everything I’ve been trying not to think about shows up at once.\n\nAnd if I do fall asleep, a lot of times I’m waking up around three or four in the morning. Not even for a clear reason. I just wake up and stare at the ceiling, and then I’m aware of every minute going by until it’s time to get up and drive to the site. I’ll tell myself not to look at the clock, but then I do, and it just makes it worse.\n\nSo yeah, I’m in bed, but I don’t feel like I’m really resting much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. More than I really want to admit.

It’s not like I walk around saying, “I’m worthless,” exactly, but that’s pretty much what it turns into in my head. I keep thinking I’ve failed everybody — my wife, my kids, the guys at work who are counting on me to keep things moving and keep them safe. And then when anything goes wrong, even something that might not totally be on me, I take it like it proves what I already suspect.

Like if I miss an appointment or forget something at home, I’m not just thinking, “Okay, I made a mistake.” I’m thinking, “Of course you did. You can’t even handle basic stuff.” If there’s a cost overrun on a job, or something slips in the schedule, or somebody flags a safety issue, I go straight to, “This is because I’m not good enough to be in charge.” I know construction always has problems — delays, money issues, things you have to solve as they come up — but lately I can’t see it that way. It all feels personal. Like every problem is evidence that I’m useless.

And then I start thinking everyone would probably be better off if I wasn’t the one responsible. My family, the crew, everybody. Not necessarily that they hate me or anything, but just… that I’m letting them down and they’d do better with someone else. It’s hard to say out loud, but yeah, I’ve been feeling pretty worthless.
